 SUMMARY OF TESTING

A) Objectives

The principal objective of this report is to summarize the testing experience on the
DM1200 SBS. Further objectives are to provide descriptions of the history of all
modifications and maintenance, methods of operation, problems and unit failures, and
performance while processing a variety of feeds, and while employing a variety of
operating methods, and to provide comparisons to the Hanford Tank Waste Treatment
and Immobilization Plant (WTP) High Level Waste (HLW) and Low Activity Waste
(LAW) Submerged Bed Scrubber (SBS) designs.

B) Test Exceptions

 This report is a summary of previously reported test results. While the source
reports were subject to various Test Exceptions, there were no Test Exceptions specific to
this report.

C) R&T Testing Conditions

 Several tests have been conducted with simulants of WTP HLW and LAW
streams on the DuraMelter 1200 (DM1200) and prototypical off-gas system since its
installation in early 2001 and the present. Throughout these tests, the off-gas system
components were operated under conditions intended to mimic WTP operating
conditions, as known at the time, in order to permit the expected performance of each
unit to be assessed. The melter and off-gas system were evaluated in the course of
producing over 180 metric tons of glass from HLW feed simulants at a maximum steady
state rate of 1450 kg/m2/day and 83 metric tons of glass from LAW feed simulants at a
maximum steady state rate of 2500 kg/m2/day.

 All of the tests were performed on the DM1200 system. The DM1200, which is
the HLW Pilot Melter, is a Joule-heated melter with Inconel 690 electrodes. The melter
shell is water-cooled and incorporates a jack-bolt thermal expansion system. The
footprint of the melter is approximately 8 ft by 6.5 ft with a 4 ft by 2.3 ft air-lift discharge
chamber appended to one end; the melter shell is almost 8 ft tall. The melt surface area
and the melt pool height are approximately 32 percent and 57 percent, respectively, of the
corresponding values for the full-scale HLW melter. The discharge riser and trough are
full-scale to verify pouring performance. The surface of the glass pool is about 1.2 m2,
and the volume is about 849 liters, corresponding to about 2 metric tons of glass. The
feed system consists of a mix tank and a feed tank, both of which are 750-gallon
polyethylene tanks with conical bottoms that are fitted with mechanical agitators. The
feed tank is also fitted with baffles to improve mixing and calibrated load cells that are
electronically monitored to determine the feed rate to the melter. The feed is introduced
into the melter using an air-displacement-slurry (ADS) pump, which is the present

 8

The Catholic University of America Summary of DM1200 SBS History and Performance
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-06R6410-2, Rev. 0

baseline for the River Protection Project Hanford Tank Waste Treatment Immobilization
Plant (RPP-WTP). Feed from the ADS pump flows into the melter through a prototypic
un-cooled feed nozzle that is located above the center of the glass pool. The melter and
entire off-gas treatment system are maintained under negative pressure by two Paxton
external induced draft blowers. This negative pressure is necessary to direct the gases
from the melter to the prototypical off-gas system. The off-gas treatment system consists
of a submerged bed scrubber (SBS); a wet electrostatic precipitator (WESP); a
high-efficiency mist eliminator (HEME for HLW only), a high-efficiency particulate air
(HEPA) filter; a thermal catalytic oxidation unit (TCO); a Selective Catalytic Reduction
(SCR) NOx removal system; a packed-bed caustic scrubber (PBS); and a second HEME.
A sulfur impregnated activated carbon column was installed between the HEPA and the
TCO for the later tests. The second HEME is used to limit entrained particle carryover
into the balance of the VSL ventilation system; the PBS and the second HEME are not
part of the WTP HLW off-gas train, which effectively ends at the SCR.

 Specific testing conditions varied according to the specific Test Plans and are
listed in each of the referenced reports. Test conditions for the SBS also varied during
testing and are detailed in the body of this report.

D) Results and Performance Against Objectives

 The SBS installed in the DM1200 off-gas system has been used in all DM1200
tests over the five-year testing period since the commissioning of the melter system. The
SBS has treated exhaust from the vitrification of over a million and half pounds of feed
producing over half a million pounds of glass. A wide range of simulated HLW and
LAW streams were treated under a variety of melter and off-gas system operating
conditions. During this period, a number of physical modifications to the SBS were made
and evaluated. From these tests, a large body of data has been collected on the
effectiveness of the SBS, and on required maintenance activities and failure modes.

 A series of physical modifications to the SBS were made during the five
year testing program to preserve the prototypical relationship with the WTP SBS as
design changes were made in response to test results. One recurring issue was the
formation of solid deposits near the base of the downcomer at the bottom of the SBS
packed column. While these deposits never fully impeded gas flow through the SBS, over
the course of five- to nine-day tests they occluded as much as 80% of the pipe area and
caused increases in the pressure differential across the SBS. None of the modifications
prior to the final configuration, including various downcomer extensions, plugging weir
tubes, and insulating the downcomer above the water level, had any effect on the
formation of downcomer solids. The final SBS modification, which opened the annulus
and shortened the submerged portion of the downcomer, eliminated the accumulation of
downcomer deposits over the last 51 days of DM1200 testing. Prior to the final SBS
modifications, occlusions were more evident in tests with HLW than LAW simulants;
however, while processing LAW Sub-Envelope A1 simulants [16], up to half the
downcomer pipe area was observed to be occluded. After the final SBS modifications,

 9

occlusions in the downcomer were not observed in tests processing either HLW or LAW
simulants [13, 19]. Further tests would be useful to verify this observation using feeds of
various compositions over a wide range of melter operating conditions.

 Throughout DM1200 testing, the SBS was periodically drained and inspected for
deposits and unusual wear. The most significant findings were accumulations of deposits
in the bottom of the SBS, the accumulation rate of which showed no evidence of
declining with increasing test duration.

 Solutions are removed from the SBS throughout each test by suction through a
wand that extends to the bottom of the SBS bowl. Over the five-year period of testing,
solutions with total solids content as high as 10,000 mg/l were processed with only one
clogging event. In solutions generated from tests processing HLW simulants, the amounts
of suspended and dissolved solids are about equal, whereas solutions generated while
processing LAW simulants have ten times higher concentrations of dissolved than
suspended solids. The composition of suspended solids is similar to that of the feed
whereas the dissolved solids consist primarily of the more volatile species.

 The effectiveness of the SBS in removing particulate ranged from DF values of 2
up to 122. Several factors have the potential to affect the efficiency of the SBS, including
the feed composition, melter operating conditions, and the rate of particle emissions from
the melter. Analysis of emissions data from tests with HLW simulants suggests that as
the flux of solids from the melter increases, so too does the SBS DF. This is not observed
in tests with LAW simulants due to the generation of higher concentrations of finer and
more soluble particles which are not as effectively removed by the SBS. In keeping with
the design of the SBS, the removal efficiencies for insoluble elements such as silicon and
iron are one or two orders of magnitude higher than those for soluble elements, such as
sodium, that form fine particles. The changes in SBS configuration that were made had
no discernable effect on particulate removal efficiency.

 The designs of the WTP HLW and LAW SBSs are similar to that of the final
configuration of the DM1200 SBS and, therefore, operational information obtained from
the DM1200 SBS during testing should be directly applicable to the WTP units.

E) Quality Requirements

 This work is a summary of previously reported test results, the quality assurance
requirements for each of which are provided in the original reports. This work was
conducted under a quality assurance program that is in place at the VSL that is based on
Nuclear Quality Assurance (NQA)-1 (1989) and NQA-2a (1990) Part 2.7. This program
is supplemented by a Quality Assurance Project Plan for RPP-WTP work that is
conducted at VSL. Test and procedure requirements by which the testing activities are
planned and controlled are also defined in this plan. The program is supported by VSL
standard operating procedures that were used for this work. This work was not subject to
DOE/RW-0333P or the requirements of the RPP-WTP Quality Assurance Project Plan

 10

for Testing Programs Generating Environmental Regulatory Data (QAPjP). However,
several of the original tests were subject to the requirements of the RPP-WTP QAPjP, as
noted in the original test reports.

F) Simulant Use

 This summary report covers testing that employed a wide range of HLW and
LAW simulants formulated using various waste bases, as detailed in the respective
original test reports. References for each waste simulant are provided in each source
report. The HLW waste simulants processed were AZ-101 [5-7, 11, 12], AZ-102 [8, 13],
C-106/AY-102 [9, 12, 13, 19], and C-104/AY-101 [10]. The LAW Sub-Envelope
simulants processed were C1 [15], A1 [16], B1 [17], A2 [19], and A and C [18].

G) Issues and Follow-On Tests

 There is no evidence that the rate of solids accumulation declines as the test
duration increases, as would be required in order to achieve a steady state condition in
which the rate of accumulation of solids is equal to the rate of removal. Instead, the rate
of solids removal showed an increasing trend with test duration. In aggregate, therefore,
the DM1200 test results indicate a build up of solids in the bottom of the SBS over time.
The limited data from tests with LAW simulants indicate that solids accumulations are
less of a problem while processing LAW simulants, presumably due to the higher
proportion of soluble components in LAW feeds. Accumulations of solids in the WTP
SBS units have the potential to interrupt operations. Further testing is needed to quantify
the rate of solids accumulations and to develop and demonstrate mitigation methods.

 Modifications to the SBS configuration over time have drastically reduced the
occlusion of the downcomer due to the accumulation of solid deposits. Further tests
would be useful to verify this observation using feeds of various compositions over a
wide range of melter operating conditions. Also, only two samples of SBS exhaust were
taken after the final modification of the SBS was completed; there are therefore only
limited data on the performance of the final configuration of the SBS unit.

 A radar level gauge (Rosemont Apex Model BC1A1SC4NO4A1OC1N1E51) was
installed on the final SBS configuration. The limited testing that was performed indicated
relatively poor performance in this particular application. Many of the performance
problems that were encountered may be due to poor reflection of the radar beam from the
turbulent surface resulting in overall decrease in measurement sensitivity, perhaps
compounded by the small size and tight geometry of the DM1200 SBS. It is therefore
recommended that application of this technology to SBS level monitoring should be
further evaluated prior to installation at the WTP.

 11

 SECTION 1.0
 INTRODUCTION

 The operational requirements for the River Protection Project – Hanford Tank
Waste Treatment & Immobilization Plant (RPP-WTP) High Level Waste (HLW) melter
system and the specific range of HLW melter feed constituents impose a number of
challenges to the off-gas treatment system. The system operates with a highly radioactive
feed and, therefore, must be very robust to limit the need for remote maintenance. The
system must effectively control and remove solid particulate matter over a wide range of
particle sizes and physical characteristics; control and remove acid gases such as SOx,
HCl, NOx in the off-gas stream; and destroy volatile organic constituents (VOCs) to
concentration levels below those imposed by regulatory requirements. In particular, the
level of radioactivity released as sub-micron particulates, notably cesium, must be
controlled to ensure an acceptable low release. A baseline design for the RPP-WTP off-
gas system was developed by BNFL, Inc. in Part B-1. It includes a submerged bed
scrubber (SBS), a wet electrostatic precipitator (WESP), and a high efficiency mist
eliminator (HEME) as the principal components [1]; a catalytic oxidation unit was added
on the basis of test data that showed that the melter alone would not provide sufficient
destruction of organics [2]. Essentially the same process train is also used for the low
activity waste (LAW) off-gas treatment system [3]. This combination of off-gas
components, while conceptually reasonable, had not been tested in an integrated manner
with a waste glass melter. Accordingly, there was a need to evaluate the performance and
design features of this combination of off-gas control components with a melter system
that is representative of the proposed RPP-WTP HLW vitrification system. Pilot scale
equipment testing is required to assure the design functions as required for the RPP-WTP
HLW vitrification systems. To address this need, the RPP-WTP deployed a one-third of
full scale HLW pilot melter system at the Vitreous State Laboratory (VSL) of The
Catholic University of America (CUA) in Washington, DC. The DuraMelter 1200
(DM1200) system was used to address a variety of objectives including collection of data
on processing rates for HLW waste compositions, and evaluation of the effectiveness of
the prototypical off-gas system components in treating exhaust from the vitrification of
both the LAW and HLW simulated waste streams. Since the commissioning of the
DM1200 in January of 2001 [4, 5], the DM1200 off-gas system has been evaluated in the
treatment of exhaust from a wide range of HLW and LAW simulated waste streams [5-
19].

 This report provides a summary of the history and performance of the SBS as a
part of the off-gas system of the DM1200, which is the HLW pilot melter facility at VSL.
Included are the design specifications, a detailed description of the unit, a complete
history of operation, listing of all modifications and repairs made to the unit, description
of all unit failures, summary of the amounts and chemical compositions of process
effluents generated by the SBS, measured particle removal efficiencies of the SBS, and
comparisons with the WTP SBS designs. The report covers a five-year period of

 12

The Catholic University of America Summary of DM1200 SBS History and Performance
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-06R6410-2, Rev. 0

operation from commissioning in 2001, during which over half a million pounds of glass
was produced from over a million and a half pounds of feed under a variety of melter and
off-gas system operating conditions. An abbreviated summary of the tests conducted, unit
performance and SBS modifications are provided in Table 1.1 and Figure 1.1. Prior to the
commissioning tests in 2001, a series of off-line tests were conducted to define operating
parameters for the SBS [20]. Reviews of the performance for the DM1200 melter and
WESP have been provided in separate reports [21, 22].

 13

 SECTION 2.0
 SBS DESCRIPTION AND DESIGN

2.1 Principle of Operation

 The SBS provides the first major processing step for the melter off-gas, which
involves contact with liquid water to cool the off-gas, condense moisture, remove large
particulates, and to scrub out soluble acid gases (e.g., HCl and HF) and dissolve soluble
particulates. The melter off-gas enters an inner central tube on the top of the SBS tank
flowing downwards, then reverses direction to flow upwards through a perforated plate
that distributes the flow into the submerged packed column. The packing provides a high
surface area for gas-liquid contact while being porous enough to allow large insoluble
particulates to settle down into the lower portion of the SBS tank. The flow reversal at the
entrance to the column also provides solids collection by impingement onto the liquid.
The solids accumulated at the bottom of the tank are kept in suspension by periodic
injection of the supernatant liquid from a manifold of four nozzles and a pump. Liquid
and suspended solids are periodically discharged from the SBS through the suction line
with a wand that extends to the bottom of the SBS sump. The saturated gas exits through
an outlet on top of the SBS.

 The SBS underwent a number of modifications after the original unit was
installed. The following sections provide details of the original unit that was installed and
the subsequent modifications that were made.

2.2 Original SBS Specifications

 The SBS installed in the DM1200 off-gas system is based on the original design
input from BNFL, Inc. obtained during Part B-1; the unit was sized to reflect the
expected gas flow rates from the DM1200. Bids were solicited from several vendors from
which the final selections were made. The construction and detailed mechanical design of
the SBS was done by Fisher-Klosterman, Inc (FKI) of Louisville, KY. Schematics of the
SBS provided in Figures 2.1 - 2.4 present technical details of the original SBS
construction. The location of the SBS in the DM1200 off-gas system is shown in Figure
2.5. Design specifications are listed in Table 2.1.

2.3 Original DM1200 SBS Design Details
 2.3.1 Process Flow and SBS Vessel

 Gas flow enters the SBS through the 4” ID inlet port and flows down the 73”-long
metal downcomer pipe in the center of the SBS vessel (see Figure 2.1). The flowing gas
contacts the water pool at its exit forming a bubble. The gas bubble forms directly below

 14

the 30” diameter diffuser plate. This plate is perforated with a large number of 3/8”
diameter holes and 1¼” Nominal Pipe Size (NPS) nozzles extending 10” below the
diffuser plate (weirs). The pressurized gas bubble formed at the outlet of the downcomer
pipe reverses the gas flow so that gas flows upward through the diffuser plate and
through a 24”-high packed column. The packing functions as the primary liquid/gas
contact surface enabling scrubbing of acid gases, the impact settling of large particulate
entrained in the gas stream, and providing surface area for water vapor condensation.
There is a 3-inch axial void above the packing to permit packing expansion due to this
gas flow. The bottom diffuser plate, a 4-mesh screen on the top, the 30” diameter outer
skirt, and the 6” inlet gas pipe on the inner and outer circumferences form the packed
column boundaries. All surfaces in contact with process fluid have been fabricated from
AL6XN (a highly corrosion resistant super-austenitic stainless steel alloy). The packing is
½” Intalox ceramic saddles (alternatively, stainless steel material has been used). This
packing type was selected by the RPP-WTP Project due to its low-pressure drop and high
void fraction. The gas flow continues upward through the flooded packed column, top
screen, and typically an additional 3-6” layer of water. The scrubbed gas is released from
the vessel water surface and flows through a 36” vertical void in the SBS upper plenum.
This void serves as a gas disengagement volume. The off-gas then exits the SBS vessel
through the 4” diameter gas discharge port on the SBS top cover. In the process, the gas
temperature is lowered to the bulk temperature of the liquid in the vessel. The SBS liquid
temperature is typically maintained in the 40-50oC range. Depending upon the incoming
flow dew point, the water vapor content in gas either increases or decreases. Evaporated
water is always replenished to maintain constant level. If condensation occurs, the
additional water gained is gravitationally discharged from the SBS through either or both
of the two 2” diameter overflow ports into the 100-gallon polyethylene SBS overflow
tank.

 The packed column outer skirt is welded to the diffuser plate. The outer skirt
extends almost 9” below the diffuser plate. This design prevents the gas bubbles from
escaping around the skirt to the outer SBS annulus and should preclude gas flow from
bypassing the packed column. The diffuser plate is a key component of the SBS design.
The two types of the incorporated diffuser plate orifices each have a different function.
The radially scattered ninety-nine (99) 3/8” holes enable direct permeation of the
incoming gas through the packed column. The one hundred and four (104) 1¼” NPS x
13.4” L "weir" pipes pass through the diffuser plate and are seal-welded to this plate.
The lower entrance is designed to accommodate liquid displaced by the forming air
bubbles as well as the return flow of liquid from the outer annulus. The upper portion of
the weir pipes (above the diffuser plate and within the packed column region) each with
seventeen (17) 3/8” holes distributes the return liquid flow to the packing.

 The 4” NPS incoming gas pipe is welded to both the SBS upper head and to the
diffuser plate. This permits the incoming pipe/packed column subassembly to be removed
as a single piece for modification or maintenance. (Note that both the SBS vessel upper
head and the lower dished head are flanged and bolted to the main cylindrical portion of
the SBS vessel for inspection and modification purposes).

 15

 The incoming gas pipe has a sealed concentric annulus formed around its exterior
by a 6” NPS pipe that extends over the 24” packed column length. Mesh insulation within
the sealed annulus prevents the flow of heat between the gas in the incoming pipe and the
counterflow of gas through the packed column. The screen forming the upper boundary
of the column is divided into four 90o segments and bolted to radial ribs. This permits
access to the packing for inspection or replacement. An additional 4” NPS gas inlet pipe
extends through the packed column on its outer edge. In the RPP-WTP baseline design,
the SBS has provision for emergency or shutdown bypass gas flow from the melter. This
provision is not utilized in the DM1200 but was included to replicate the geometry of the
full-scale system. The pipe inlet flange, located on the upper SBS vessel head is therefore
“blanked-out.”

 2.3.2 Heat Removal System

 The SBS system has two alternative means of removing the heat released when
cooling the incoming off gas. The first is a cooling jacket that is an integral portion of the
SBS vessel. The second is an external, forced-circulation cooling system using a coolant
recycle pump and a plate/frame heat exchanger. Both systems have heat transfer capacity
in excess of the nominal 250,000 BTU/hr design basis. The cooling jacket system is
comparable to the RPP-WTP design basis and is normally used. The external cooling
loop system serves as a back up.

 The cooling jacket surrounds the lower portion of the SBS vessel. Heat transfer is
by natural convection of the water within the SBS (secondary loop) and by forced
convection of chilled service water through the external jacket (primary loop). The SBS
water flows through the SBS external annulus formed by the packed column skirt and the
SBS vessel wall. Natural convection of the SBS water flowing past the internal SBS wall
and conduction through the vessel wall transfers heat to a circulating coolant in the
jacket. The vessel wall is either cooled by chilled water/ethylene glycol mixture at ~25oF
or filtered service water at ~50oF. The coolant flows through axial passages formed in the
cooling jacket. Filtered fresh water, when used as a secondary coolant, typically has a 25-
30oF higher inlet temperature. The corresponding mean temperature difference between
the coolant and SBS water is lowered and this lowers the heat transfer rate through the
jacket. The cooling jacket has a 48” inner wall diameter and a 36” height (for a total heat-
transfer surface area of 37.3 ft²). The coolant channel annulus formed is ½” wide. A total
of 8 axial dividers serve as both a structural stiffener for the jacket and also form a series
of axial flow channels increasing the coolant velocity and hence the forced convection
film coefficient. The coolant water from the VSL building supply enters at a single 1”
port in the lower portion of the jacket. The flow then divides passing around in 180o
loops in opposing circumferential directions around the SBS vessel. The coolant traverses
vertically in a zigzag pattern as it flows through each of the 9 cooling channels formed.
Ultimately, the coolant flow exits through a single 1” port located in the upper portion of
the jacket. The three-way diverter valve incorporated in this system enables automatic

 16

control of the coolant flow in the primary loop to ensure stable liquid temperature within
the SBS vessel.

 The external cooling system utilizes overflow liquid which transits from the SBS
vessel overflow line, in sequence, to the overflow tank, the recycle pump, the secondary
side of the plate/frame heat exchanger and back to the SBS vessel. Chilled building water
is also used as a primary coolant for the plate/frame heat exchanger. The Jordan
regulating valve ensures constant SBS overflow tank water temperature by automatically
controlling the coolant water inlet flow rate. The recycle flow of overflow liquid can also
be used in the water pulsing system for keeping particulate collected in the SBS vessel in
suspension. The inlet flow to the recycle pump passes through a dual strainer. The
strainer serves to minimize the potential for clogging at the pump inlet.

 Detailed heat and energy balances around the SBS have been reported previously
for tests with moist air [20], exhaust generated from HLW AZ-101 simulated waste [11],
exhaust generated from HLW C-106/AY-102 simulated waste [12] and exhaust generated
from LAW Sub-Envelope C1 simulated waste [18] and therefore will not be reported
here. A summary of the amount of energy supplied by the melter electrodes that is
exchanged by the SBS is given in Table 2.2. Approximately, half the heat generated by
the electrodes is dissipated by the SBS, with the balance being lost through the melter
cooling panels, the discharged glass, the transition line, the SBS exhaust, and the melter
skin.

 2.3.3 Liquid Recycle and Timed Water Lance System

 This system consists of four water jet pulsing lances and a suction lance
terminating in a rectangular wand for collection of accumulated solids. The system
function is to agitate the particulate matter accumulating in the lower portion of the SBS
bottom head. The recycle pump diverts some of the recycle flow to each of four nozzles.
Each nozzle is located at the end of 1” PVC pipes positioned at 90o intervals
circumferentially along the inner sidewalls of the SBS vessel. The nozzles are positioned
at a 15o angle to the tangent to the wall. When the water pulse is actuated, the water
flowing through the nozzle causes the SBS liquid to form a "whirlpool" which agitates
and moves any settling particulate and keeps it in suspension. It also focuses the
particulate towards the central region of the SBS vessel lower dished head. A suction
wand (see Fig. 2.4) is positioned centrally near the bottom of the SBS dished head.
Periodically water is drawn from the SBS vessel through this suction wand and piped to
the SBS sample tank. The frequency and duration of the water pulsing system is
controlled by an air-actuated pinch valve system. An electronic timer and air solenoid
valve system enables individual control of each pinch valve. The system can be
programmed for different durations of the water pulse in various sequences. The system
can be set to run either continuously or as a single pulse cycle. When the water nozzles
are not pulsing, the recycle water returns to the SBS overflow tank. The sequence used
throughout melter testing subsequent to the commissioning tests was determined during

 17

The Catholic University of America Summary of DM1200 SBS History and Performance
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-06R6410-2, Rev. 0

off-line tests [20]. The jets were activated hourly as four sequences of 10-second
activations with pauses of 30 seconds in between.

 2.3.4 Liquid Addition/Removal System

 Liquid can be removed from the SBS system by three different means. First,
water can be directly pumped from the SBS vessel to the SBS sample tank using the
suction lance described previously. Second, there are two bottom drain lines located in
the SBS vessel dished head. Water from these drain lines will normally be sent to the
SBS sample tank. Third, water from the SBS overflow tank can be pumped to the SBS
sample tank. During special tests and maintenance operations, all three of these lines can
be used. The utility service water line can be used to fill the SBS vessel or the overflow
tank.

2.4 Modifications to the Original SBS Configuration
 2.4.1 Modifications Based on Results from Off-Line Testing

 Several modifications were made to the SBS as a result of data collected during
initial stand-alone testing using a Landa evaporator as an off-gas/steam source simulator
[20] and subsequent testing by feeding the DM1200 melter with water; these are briefly
summarized here for background. Significant among the issues were:

 • Fluctuations in melter pressure (about 2 in. W.C.) attributed to oscillations of the
 large gas bubble that forms underneath of the SBS diffuser plate at the exit of the
 downcomer pipe.

 • Rapid disintegration of the ceramic saddle packing media that was originally
 specified by the RPP-WTP Project.

 • A need for supplemental cooling to handle the increased steam flow into the SBS
 at the higher processing rates that are realized when the glass pool is agitated by
 bubbling.

 Several modifications were tested, including extension of the center inlet
downcomer below the diffuser plate and alternative packing media. A perforated
downcomer extension tube extending 12 inches below the diffuser plate was installed
with holes along the tube wall with the intent of breaking up the gas bubble forming
below the diffuser plate. This change reduced the amplitude of the pressure pulsations to
within about 0.5 in W.C. Several other changes were also investigated, such as plugging
the weir tubes, plugging the standby gas nozzle, and constraining the inlet source of gas.
However, the perforated downcomer extension appeared to have the most effect in
mitigating the pressure fluctuations. In the downcomer extension used during the

 18

commissioning tests, there were 32 half-inch diameter holes distributed along the tube in
four rows of eight, 1½ inches apart.

 Various packing media, including ½ inch stainless steel saddles, metal balls, and
ceramic balls were tested in place of the original ceramic Intallox saddle packing
medium. Based on these tests, ceramic balls of approximately an inch in diameter
(Denstone-80, later changed to Denstone-99), packed all the way up to the upper
retaining screen were used during the commission testing. However, the original Intallox
saddles were retained in the spaces between the liquid weirs that protruded through the
diffuser plate to prevent jamming of the ceramic balls into crevices. A metal screen was
laid on top of the weirs to eliminate intermixing of packing media.

 2.4.2 Modifications During AZ-101 Tests [6]

 Prior to this test series, the cooling system was modified to increase capacity. A
schematic diagram of the modified SBS cooling system is given in Figure 2.6. An
external heat exchanger and an internal cooling coil were added in order to decrease the
operating temperature of the SBS and minimize downstream moisture condensation; the
cooling coil is connected to the heat exchanger. This was in addition to the existing outer
cooling jacket on the SBS, which alone proved insufficient for the higher glass
production rate tests.

 After four tests, the SBS downcomer was redesigned and a new downcomer was
installed at the request of the Project. The modification was done in response to solids
deposition on the inside of the downcomer at the point where it passes through the
diffuser plate. A potential reason for solids accumulation at this location was thought to
be the slightly raised metal lugs used to position the downcomer when it was appended to
the original SBS design. However, the new design, which provided a smooth, flush inside
surface for the downcomer did not eliminate the solids deposition at that point. The new
downcomer, before and after installation, is shown in Figure 2.7. The flat-bottom
downcomer was used for Tests 1-4 and the Commissioning Tests [5], while the serrated-
bottom downcomer was used for Tests 5-9 and several subsequent tests [7, 8, 15-17].

 2.4.3 Modifications During AZ-101 Tests [7]

 The weir tubes on the bottom of the SBS were plugged during the first two tests
of this test series to determine the effect on solids accumulation and operation of the SBS.
The tubes were plugged with rubber stoppers, as shown in Figure 2.8. At the end of the
two tests, the SBS was drained and the bowl was dropped to reveal that all the stoppers
remained in place during testing. The stoppers were then removed, revealing large
accumulations of solids filling the weir tubes. The weir tubes were kept open in all
subsequent tests.

 19

The Catholic University of America Summary of DM1200 SBS History and Performance
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-06R6410-2, Rev. 0

           2.4.4   Modifications for C-106/AY-102 [9] and C-104/AY-101 [10] Tests

 The downcomer extension in place for several of the previous tests was removed
for these two tests.

 2.4.5 Modifications during Reductant [12] and Configuration [11] Tests

 Another design variation of the SBS downcomer extension pipe was installed
prior to these tests in an attempt to reduce the solids occlusion experienced in many
previous tests. Views of downcomer bottom before and after addition of the flared
extension piece or “crown” are provided in Figure 2.9.

 After the conclusion of Test 2 (6/06/03), diagnostic tests indicated that a weld
sealing the pre-existing lower sleeve to the outside of the lower portion of the downcomer
(through the packed bed) was cracked, essentially allowing this sleeve to fill with the
SBS fluid. Figure 2.10(a) shows this lower sleeve. This weld was repaired, and a pressure
tap was permanently installed to provide an indication of any future leaks. Figure 2.10(b)
shows the new insulating sleeve that was installed on the downcomer, which insulates the
downcomer from the top of the packed bed to the top lid of the SBS shell (i.e., above the
level of the pre-existing lower sleeve). At the time of the installation of the upper sleeve,
15.1 kg of packing was added to the top of the SBS bed.

2.5.       Final SBS Configuration (March 2004) [11, 13, 19]

 The SBS was extensively modified prior to the test conducted on 3/23/04. The
significant modifications to the SBS are:

 • The diameter of the bed was increased by 20% to make the superficial flow
 velocity closer to its prototypical value.

 • The water level above the packing was lowered by several inches such that the
 nominal level is now equal to the prototypical level of 3 inches above the top of
 the bed packing. This change reduces the pressure drop across the SBS. It also
 results in a prototypical SBS plenum height (21 inches) from the top of the liquid
 to the off-gas entry and exit points. The DM1200 SBS plenum volume is,
 however, smaller than prototypical due to the difference in SBS diameters.

 • The overflow nozzle design was changed to the prototypical funnel-like shape.

 • The downcomer annular pipe was changed to the prototypical open-ended type.

 • An additional ring of the inner cooling coil was added, making a total of two coils

 20

 in the DM1200 system as compared to three in the WTP SBS. This change
 improves the heat transfer efficiency, enabling the support of higher feed rates to
 the melter.

 Figure 2.11 shows a schematic side view of the modified SBS internals. The gas
inlet labeled A1 is redundant and is normally not used. The horizontal dotted line above
the cooling coils indicates the nominal water level. Photographs of the new SBS before
installation are shown in Figures 2.12 to 2.15. Figure 2.12 provides an overall view,
Figures 2.13 and 2.14 provide views of the diffuser plate from the top and bottom, and
Figure 2.15 provides a view of the top of the packing grate. The new downcomer exterior
outfitted with thermocouples is shown in Figure 2.16. In addition to the physical
modifications of the SBS, the cooling piping and heat exchanger system serving the SBS
also were modified.

 21

 SECTION 3.0
 HISTORY OF SBS OPERATION, MAINTENANCE
 AND MODIFICATIONS

3.1 Chronology of SBS Usage and Maintenance

 The SBS installed in the DM1200 off-gas system has been used in all DM1200
tests since the commissioning of the melter system in January 2001. The SBS has treated
exhaust from the vitrification of over a million and half pounds of feed producing over
half a million pounds of glass. A wide range of simulated HLW and LAW streams were
treated under a variety of melter conditions including high bubbling rates and elevated
melt temperatures. During the five-year testing period, melter feeds were spiked with
various compounds including hazardous organics, cesium, selenium, halides, and
sulfates, which are not fully retained in the glass and are not fully removed from the
exhaust stream by the SBS. The unit worked well over five years with no interruption to
testing attributable to SBS failures. No problems were encountered with overall pressure
control or in maintaining negative pressure on the melter as a result of SBS pressure/flow
oscillations. However, chronic problems that were encountered, such as build up of
deposits in the downcomer and in the bowl, may well be of concern for long-term,
continuous operation at WTP. A complete chronology of SBS operations, maintenance,
and modifications is given in Table 3.1.

3.2 Monitored Parameters

 The parameters monitored and recorded electronically through testing are
summarized in Table 3.2. SBS inlet and outlet gas temperatures, pressure drop across the
SBS, SBS water temperature, heat exchanger inlet and outlet water temperatures, and
heat exchanger flow rates were measured and recorded electronically every two minutes
during all the tests. Additional measurements of gas temperatures along the length of the
downcomer, and downcomer annulus pressure were added in the final SBS configuration
beginning in March of 2004. Examples of these data over the course of a test while
operating with the final SBS configuration are provided in Figures 3.1 - 3.8.

 The SBS outlet gas temperature is determined by the manner in which the SBS is
operated. SBS outlet gas temperatures ranged from a target low of about 25°C in early
tests to a target high of about 55°C in later tests. The target outlet temperature for most of
the tests was 40°C [6-12, 15-18]; however, in later tests, temperatures of 50-55°C were
targeted [11, 13, 19]. The SBS inlet and outlet gas temperatures plotted in Figure 3.1
show downward spikes in SBS inlet gas temperature towards the beginning of the run due
to periodic cleaning of the film cooler with water, a power failure at 67.1 hours, and a
period of targeting an SBS outlet temperature of 55°C. Even though operation of the SBS

 22

at a temperature higher than 50°C was possible, long-term operation at this higher
temperature was a problem in the DM1200 system due to condensation and higher than
desirable temperatures in a number of the downstream off-gas components. During
steady state operations for most test segments, the inlet gas temperatures ranged between
200 and 300°C; lower temperatures were observed in test segments with very low feed
rates [6, 18, 19] and while the film cooler was being cleaned, whereas higher
temperatures were observed in test segments with very high feed rates [11, 13] and
periods of lower cold cap coverage, such as occurs at the beginning of tests. The inlet,
outlet, and differential pressures illustrated in Figure 3.2 show a constant pressure drop
across the SBS of about 31 in. W.C and a dip in inlet and outlet pressure at ≈24 hours due
to a partial film cooler blockage and related cleaning. In tests conducted prior to the final
SBS modification, the total pressure drop was about 10 in. W.C higher due to the
differences in submerged downcomer length; increases in differential pressure were
commonly observed as a result of downcomer blockages, as illustrated in Figure 3.9.
Off-gas temperatures in the downcomer measured at 12 depths (from 3 to 58 inches at
5-inch intervals) and SBS sump water temperatures depicted in Figure 3.4 show the
progressive cooling of the exhaust stream as it flows through the SBS. In the final SBS
configuration, the SBS sump solutions were only about 5°C cooler than the SBS exhaust
temperature, indicating that the SBS effectively quenches the gas stream, with the liquid
and gas phases coming close to reaching thermal equilibrium, as illustrated in Figure 3.5.

 Cooling of the melter exhaust is accomplished by heat exchange with cooled
water supplied to internal cooling coils and an external jacket. The SBS outlet gas
temperatures are regulated by adjusting the flow rate of water into these units. Water
temperatures at the SBS inner cooling coil inlet, inner cooling coil outlet/jacket inlet, and
jacket outlet given in Figure 3.6 show a large water temperature difference of about 25°C
across the SBS inner cooling coil and only about a 1-2°C temperature difference across
the jacket. The upward spike in the cooling coil inlet water temperature is due to the
building water chiller being off-line between 76.0 and 76.6 hours. The amounts of heat
removed by the SBS inner coil and jacket illustrated in Figure 3.8 show about 114 kW
heat removed by the SBS inner cooling coil and about 7 kW by the cooling jacket, hence
about 94% of the heat load to the SBS was removed by the inner cooling coil and about
6% by the cooling jacket. In tests conducted prior to the final SBS modification, the
capacity of the internal coil (plate heat exchanger) was half that of the final configuration
and, therefore, the jacket took a greater proportion of the heat duty. The water
temperatures and heat duties for a test conducted prior to the SBS modifications are given
in Figures 3.10 and 3.11. In keeping with other tests conducted with this SBS
configuration, about 73% of the heat load to the SBS was removed by the plate heat
exchanger and about 27% by the cooling jacket.

3.3 Failures and Malfunctions

 The most significant concern over the course of testing was the occlusion of the
SBS downcomer prior to the final modification of the SBS [5-11, 15-17]. The deposits in

 23

the downcomer were never sufficient to completely interrupt gas flow through the
DM1200 SBS over the test durations employed; however, the tendency for clogging is of
concern for the continuously operated WTP units. Table 3.3 provides a list of all the
downcomer blockages observed with various cameras, post-test inspections, and
evidenced by an increase in the pressure drop across the downcomer for each test.
Representative pictures of the occlusions are given in Section 4.3. The majority of the
SBS modifications were made in efforts to eliminate the formation of the deposits near
the air-water interface at the bottom of the downcomer. Prior to the final SBS
modification in March of 2004, none of the modifications shown in Figure 1.1, including
the various downcomer extensions (flat bottom with side perforations, saw tooth bottom
with side perforations, no extension, and the crown), insulation of the upper sleeve, and
plugging the weir tubes, prevented the formation of deposits inside the downcomer.
Conversely, the opening of the annulus in the final SBS modification drastically reduced
the appearance of downcomer deposits. Notice in Table 3.3, that the last two testing
series listed [13, 19] had less than one percent of occlusions observed in the downcomer
and very low increases in differential pressure across the downcomer during each test.
This observation extended over about 51 days of testing while about 65 metric tons of
glass was produced. It should be noted, however, that about a third of the run time was
devoted to the MACT tests [19] in which production rates were deliberately lower in
order to achieve a plenum temperature target and, therefore, were not a rigorous test for
the SBS.

 The wand that extends to the bottom of the SBS bowl to collect solids clogged for
a period of time during a preliminary LAW MACT test [19]. The unit had been idle for
approximately two and half months prior to the preliminary test. Periodic attempts were
made to remove the clogging by applying water pressure and, in the meantime, the SBS
was blown down by opening valves in the SBS vessel or overflow tank. After about 75
hours of runtime, attempts to remove the clogging were successful, and normal use of the
wand for blow-downs continued. The clogging was likely a result of the wand having sat
in the accumulated solids in the bottom of the SBS bowl during the idle time; however,
idling times of this duration have occurred frequently between past tests without causing
the wand to clog. A key difference between this and prior idling periods was the lack of
mechanical removal (by completely draining the SBS, dropping the bowl, and scooping
out the solids) of solids between tests. Post-test inspections showed that the amount of
solids accumulated was greater than had been the case prior to all previous mechanical
cleanings, as a direct result of the deliberately longer duration between the cleaning
events. The amount of solids accumulated indicates that the rate of solids accumulation
shows no evidence of decreasing with time between cleanings (see Section 4.4).

 The pressure sensor lines used to detect differential pressure across the unit
clogged frequently, requiring cleaning during testing. The frequency was not as high as
observed in the film cooler and transition line [21]; however, manual verification of a line
status was required each time operators noticed increases in the pressure drop across the
SBS.

 24

The Catholic University of America Summary of DM1200 SBS History and Performance
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-06R6410-2, Rev. 0

3.4 Radar Detector for Monitoring Liquid Level in SBS

 The radar level gauge (Rosemont Apex Model BC1A1SC4NO4A1OC1N1E51)
installed on the final SBS configuration was a unit previously used on the DM3300 LAW
Pilot Melter system. The instrument was found to be faulty upon arrival at VSL and was
therefore sent to the manufacturer for repair. It was received back at VSL in March 2004
and put into service in June 2004. The installation details are shown in Figure 3.12. The
equipment location on the SBS lid plate is indicated in Figure 2.11.

 Testing of the radar gauge was limited, as was the amount of useful data that was
obtained. Reading stability issues were observed that were apparently caused by the
presence of the large metallic mass of the inner vessel in the center of the SBS. The lack
of available space on the SBS lid precluded relocation of the unit. Eventually, after much
tuning, stable readings were obtained when the liquid level in the SBS was flat (i.e., with
the SBS off-line). Those readings were referenced to the sight-glass level indicator,
which had a distance scale marked from the lowest point on the inner surface of the SBS
bottom dish (71.5±0.5 inch at the overflow drain level). The response of the radar gauge
to liquid level changes was verified by increasing the liquid level by ~4 inches above the
overflow level and was found to be accurate to within ±0.5 inch (within ±1 division on
the sight-glass scale). Lowering the liquid level below the overflow caused instrument
readings to become increasingly unstable and erratic once the metal screen covering the
inner vessel was exposed (the overflow level is only 3 inches above the screen).

 A typical set of data obtained from the radar level gauge is plotted in Figure 3.13.
The data are noisy with typical variability of about 1 inch. This is not surprising since the
liquid level is very turbulent during normal operation. The frequent, larger (up to 2 inch)
fluctuations downwards may be indicative of the occasional exposure of the inner vessel
screen to the radar beam. The average level from data in Figure 3.13 is about 73.25
inches, which would be nearly 2 inches above the overflow level. This could be a result
of foaming but also could be due to poor reflection of the radar beam from the turbulent
surface, resulting in a decrease in the sensitivity1 to level change. If the latter is true, it
may be a concern with respect to the performance of the unit in this particular application
because of the difficulties posed by on-line (i.e. with a turbulent surface) calibration.

 At the end of Test 2B [13] the electronic integrator board in the gauge failed and
required replacement. However, in view of the replacement cost, the long lead time for
the factory repair, and the limited value of the data, the unit was taken out of service and
no further testing was performed.

1
  The manufacturer’s Product Manual (00809-0100-4731, Rosemount, Inc., 2000, pp. 4-27 – 4-29) clearly
stresses that this may affect the unit’s “Reference Gauge Height” setting and thus the output.

 25

 SECTION 4.0
 SBS SOLUTIONS AND DEPOSITS

4.1 SBS Solutions

 One-liter samples were collected from the SBS sump each time liquids were
blown down and at the end of each test. Selected samples were subjected to total
dissolved solids (TDS) and total suspended solids (TSS) determinations by gravimetric
analysis of filtered material and the evaporated filtrate. An additional sample was filtered
to generate solids and filtrate for complete chemical analysis, which included pH
determination, direct current plasma - atomic emission spectroscopy (DCP-AES) analysis
for metals, atomic absorption (AA) for cesium, ion selective electrode (ISE) for
ammonium, and ion chromatography (IC) for all other anions; the dried filtered solids
underwent microwave-assisted acid dissolution prior to chemical analysis. The only
anions determined in the filtered solids were sulfate and iodide due to interference from
the acids required to dissolve the filtered solids. A complete list of the total solution
volume removed from the SBS during each test is provided in Table 4.1; the data are
plotted versus the amount of water fed into the melter in Figure 4.1. Chemical analysis of
SBS solutions from the end of many of the tests is provided in Table 4.2.

 The amount of liquid accumulated in the SBS during each test is determined by
the SBS sump temperature set-point and the feed rate of water into the SBS, as illustrated
in Figure 4.2. In early tests that targeted SBS temperatures below 30°C, almost all of the
feed water was condensed in the SBS [5, 6]. Less of the feed water was condensed in the
SBS as the sump temperature set-point was increased to 40°C and subsequently to 55°C.
The differences in the percentage of feed water condensed at the same SBS exhaust
temperatures are attributed to the differences in water feed rate to the melter. For
example, tests processing feed at low rates due to the lack of bubbling (i.e., 10/07/02 -
10/12/02 [7]) resulted in higher condensation rates than tests targeting the same SBS
exhaust temperature with faster feed processing rates due to bubbling (i.e., 10/15/02 -
10/24/02 [7]).

 The SBS solution pH values are dependent on the composition of melter feed
being vitrified, as illustrated in Figure 4.3. Mildly acidic SBS solutions result from
processing LAW simulants which contain high concentrations of volatile components
that form acid gases. Solutions do not become more acidic due to high concentrations of
alkali from the melter and ammonium generated from the decomposition of sugar and
nitrates, which neutralizes some of the acids. Mildly basic solutions result from
processing HLW simulants which contain low concentrations of volatile acidic
components in the feed. Exceptions are HLW feed that is nitrated or contains significant
concentrations of elements which form anionic species in the solution such as tellurium
and selenium [6, 12]. Solutions generated from these feeds are often more acidic than

 26

solutions generated while processing LAW simulants due to the lower buffering capacity
from the relative lack of alkali and ammonium.

 Dissolved and suspended solids concentrations in SBS solutions each ranged from
a few hundred to five to six thousand mg/l. The solids and chemical content of the SBS
solutions are dependent on the melter feed composition, feed processing rate, and melt
pool bubbling. The SBS removes the majority of the coarse particles, which tend to be
mostly insoluble materials and only a fraction of the fine and water soluble particles. The
dissolved and suspended solids concentrations for samples from the end of each test
compared in Figures 4.4 and 4.5 demonstrate these trends for tests with HLW and LAW
simulants, respectively. The ratio of suspended to dissolved solids for SBS solutions
generated while processing LAW simulants is lower due to the higher proportion of
soluble components in the feed and the use of multiple bubbling outlets, which reduces
particulate entrainment. The concentrations of suspended and dissolved solids for SBS
solutions generated while processing HLW simulants are often very similar due to the
relative lack of volatiles in the HLW simulant and the aggressive bubbling through fewer
bubblers required for achieving production rates with HLW feeds. Tests with HLW
simulants and low glass production rates, corresponding to lower glass pool bubbling
rates, have higher proportions of dissolved solids due to the lower entrainment of
insoluble species in the melter exhaust. The solids content of the SBS solutions increases
with the particulate emission rate from the melter, as illustrated in Figure 4.6. The melter
emissions in turn are a function of feed composition, bubbler configuration, and bubbler
air flow rate [21].

 Representitive examples of the SBS dissolved and suspended solids compositions
are illustrated for samples from HLW and LAW tests in Figures 4.7 and 4.8. The
dissolved solids consist mainly of species such as halogens, boron, selenium, and alkali
metals in solutions generated while processing HLW simulants. Dissolved solids
generated from tests processing LAW simulants contain the same constituents, as well as
nitrite, nitrate, sulfate, and ammonium, which often constitute greater than half the
dissolved SBS solids in the LAW melter tests due to the high feed concentrations of
nitrate/nitrite and the sugar additions. The suspended solids more closely resemble the
feed and consist primarily of major feed components such as iron, silicon, aluminum,
sodium, and zinc, as well as enrichments of selenium.

4.2 Estimates of Accumulations in SBS Fluids

 Estimates of elemental accumulations in the SBS blow-down solutions for select
tests are provided in Table 4.3. The accumulation totals are the products of the chemical
analyses and the total accumulated liquid volumes. Single sample concentrations from the
end of each test were used for the estimates. The accumulations given, therefore, are
mostly upper estimates since the concentration values were taken near the end of tests
and the concentrations certainly increased over the course of the test segment. They do
not include any solid residue in the SBS bowl or on the downcomer (see Sections 4.3 and

 27

4.4). The accumulations estimated from blow-down data are also compared to estimates
calculated from test average emissions data as percent of feed for select tests. The
accumulated mass of the various constituents in SBS solutions is affected by the duration
of the testing period, the concentration of the constituent in the feed, the volatility of the
constituent, and conditions in the melter. Over a kilogram of elements abundant in the
feed, such as boron, silicon, iron, and sodium, was estimated to be present in the SBS
blow-down solutions for many testing intervals. Volatile species such as halogens,
sulfate, selenium, nitrogen oxides, and arsenic had accumulated masses greater than a
kilogram for many intervals even though their concentrations in the feed were low.
Ammonium, although not a feed component, was present in large quantities in SBS
solutions as a decomposition product of feed nitrogen oxides and sugar. Agreement
between the two methods of estimating accumulations in the SBS (analyzed solution
concentration × accumulated liquid volume vs. feed percent calculated from emissions
data) was reasonable for many constituents in most tests in view of the fact that the off-
gas samples represent only a one- to three-hour period whereas the solution analysis
integrates over a much longer time period. Both estimation methods show higher
accumulation rates as a percentage of HLW feed in the later tests with higher bubbling
rates [8-13] than in the early tests with lower bubbling rates [6,7]. As expected, volatile
elements such as sulfur, halogens, and selenium are present in SBS solutions at high
percentages of feed. Cesium was present at 0.6 to 13% of feed in SBS solutions from
tests processing LAW simulants.

4.3 Deposits in the SBS Downcomer

 Throughout most of the DM1200 testing, deposits in the downcomer were
observed and documented using a variety of methods including cameras, post-test
inspection, and increases in differential pressure across the downcomer (see Section 3.3
and Table 3.3). Representative pictures of these deposits are given in Figures 4.9 – 4.11.
Notice that each deposit formed during tests with different downcomer extensions
indicating that the extension or lack of an extension had little bearing on the formation of
the deposits. The pictures illustrate deposits for only two different feed types; however,
deposits formed during tests with all four HLW waste compositions tested. Also, notice
that the deposits occurred near the bottom of the downcomer, near the air-water interface.
The extent of occlusion ranges from less than one percent in tests using the final SBS
configuration to over eighty percent of the pipe area, as summarized in Table 3.3. Figure
4.12 shows the downcomer free of deposits after the last test conducted on the DM1200.

 The composition of the downcomer deposits was determined by dissolving select
samples in acid followed by DCP-AES analysis and also by SEM-EDS techniques. The
results shown in Table 4.4 indicate the composition of the deposits to be similar to the
composition of solutions in the SBS. The compositions are similar to the feed with
respect to major components such as silicon, aluminum, and iron with enrichments over
feed concentrations of volatile elements, most notably selenium and tellurium. This
suggests that the deposits are formed by the drying or caking of solutions at the base of

 28

the downcomer, rather than the formation of the deposits by segregation of specific
constituents onto the downcomer.

4.4 Deposits in the SBS Bowl

 The amount of material accumulating in the bottom of the SBS was determined
on a number of occasions over the five year span of DM1200 tests. This was
accomplished during idling periods by draining the SBS of all liquids, unbolting the
bottom bowl from the body of the SBS, and lowering the bowl several feet using jacks.
The bowl was subsequently photographed and the sludge in the bowl was removed,
weighed, and analyzed for a variety constituents. Representative pictures of the bowl with
solids from the end of various tests are given in Figures 4.13 – 4.17. The amount of dried
solids removed at select intervals during DM1200 testing is listed in Table 4.5. Similar to
the downcomer deposits, significant amounts of solids accumulated in the bottom of the
bowl for all HLW waste types and the amount was not impacted by the type of
downcomer extension used during testing. Unlike the downcomer deposits, the final
modification to the SBS did not noticeably affect the accumulation of solids in the bottom
of the SBS, as shown in Figure 4.17. Notice in several of the figures that the square
suction wand used to remove solids from the bottom of the bowl is partially submerged in
the SBS solids. Over time this may clog the wand preventing the removal of solutions
with suspended solids from the bottom of the bowl; this is thought to be the origin of a
clog that occurred during an idling period (see Section 3.3).

 Table 4.5 also shows the rate of solids accumulation obtained by dividing the
mass of solids accumulated by the number of run days for that accumulation. If a steady
state is reached in which the rate of solids removal from the sump fluid blow-downs
approaches the rate of solids collection from the off-gas stream, the net accumulation rate
should approach zero in the limit of large run durations. Accordingly, Figure 4.18 shows
a plot of the solids accumulation rate versus run duration. There is no indication that the
solids accumulation rate decreases with run time and, in fact, the trend is an increase with
run time. Furthermore, the points with the lowest accumulation rates are for tests with
HLW feeds without bubbling and for LAW tests, both of which would be expected to be
lower than for HLW feeds with bubbling. Without these points, the increase is even more
apparent. Such behavior could be an issue during continuous operations. The limited data
from tests with LAW simulants indicate that solids accumulations are less of a problem
while processing LAW simulants, presumably due to the higher proportion of soluble
components in LAW feeds.

 In two tests with the weir tubes plugged [7], over sixteen kilograms of wet solids
was taken from the weir tubes after the plugs were removed. This solids accumulation in
the weir tubes plus 17 kilograms of wet solids in the bottom of the bowl and another
kilogram of solids in the bottom drain pipe suggested that plugging the weir tubes may
have increased solids deposition in the SBS. The differential pressure across the SBS
downcomer for the test with plugged weir tubes increased from a test segment average of

 29

The Catholic University of America Summary of DM1200 SBS History and Performance
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-06R6410-2, Rev. 0

41.5 to 46.8 inches water. In other tests conducted with the same SBS configuration, test
segment average pressure drops ranged from 38.3 to 46.6 inches water; thus, the pressure
drops for the tests with the weir tubes plugged were within the range of those for tests
without the tubes plugged.

 The composition of the SBS bowl deposits was determined by dissolving select
samples in acid followed by DCP-AES analysis as well as by SEM-EDS techniques. The
results shown in Table 4.4 indicate the composition of the deposits to be similar to the
composition of solutions in the SBS. The compositions are similar to the feed and
suspended solids in SBS solutions with respect to major components such as silicon,
aluminum, and iron, with enrichments over feed concentrations of volatile elements, most
notably selenium and tellurium. This suggests that the deposits are collected uniformly
from the suspended solids in SBS solutions rather than by the preferential settling out of
specific constituents.

 30

 SECTION 5.0
 SBS PARTICULATE REMOVAL PERFORMANCE

 Exhaust samples were taken at sampling points located between various off-gas
system components using 40-CFR-60 Methods 3, 5, and 29 to determine particulate
removal efficiencies of these components, including the SBS exhaust. Sampling durations
were up to one hour for the melter and one to three hours for the SBS exhaust. Teflon
filters were used to allow for analysis of all feed components. The majority of the off-gas
analyte concentrations were derived from laboratory data on solutions extracted from air
samples (filters and various solutions) together with measurements of the volume of air
sampled. The volume of air sampled and the rate at which it can be sampled are defined
in 40-CFR-60 and SW-846. Isokinetic sampling, which entails removing gas from the
exhaust at the same velocity that the air is flowing in the duct (40-CFR-60, Methods 1-5),
was used. Total particulate loading was determined by gravimetric analysis of the
standard particle filter and of probe-rinse solutions. Downstream of the particulate filter
in the sampling train are iced impingers with acidic (5% concentrated nitric acid plus
10% hydrogen peroxide) and basic (2 N sodium hydroxide) solutions. The analysis of
these solutions permits the determination of total gaseous emissions of several elements,
notably halides and sulfur. An additional dissolution procedure was required for analysis
of ruthenium in particulate samples [12]. Undissolved ruthenium particles were filtered
from the nitric/hydrofluoric acid digestate, fused with sodium hydroxide, and dissolved
with hydrochloric acid for analysis. Results from all inorganic isokinetic samples taken at
the SBS outlet are provided in Table 5.1, including test, feed type, melt pool bubbling
rate, SBS elemental emission rates, concurrent melter emission rates, and SBS
decontamination factors (DFs). Notice the distinction that is made between constituents
sampled as particles and as "gas". The "gaseous" constituents are operationally defined as
those species that are scrubbed in the impinger solutions after the air stream has passed
through a 0.3 µm heated filter. Impinger solutions from off-gas sampling were analyzed
for all of the elements in the feed but only halides, boron, selenium, and sulfur were
detected. The amount of particulate collected on sampling filters was not sufficient to
conduct both metals and total halide analysis and, therefore, sample analyses only
account for gaseous chlorine and fluorine.

 The effectiveness of the SBS at removing particulate ranged from DF values of 2
up to 122, as summarized in Table 5.2. The SBS is intended to remove coarse, insoluble
particles that would be formed by entrainment into the melter exhaust of the majority of
the feed components. The solids DF across the SBS therefore would be affected by the
amount and type of solids in the melter emissions. Illustrated in Figure 5.1 is a trend of
increasing DF across the SBS with increasing total particulate flux from the melter. The
DF values do not increase with input from the melter for exhaust generated while
processing LAW simulants due to the higher alkali, sulfur, and halide content since these
species form fine particles that are not as effectively removed by the SBS. The three
samples with the highest DF values (113-122) are all from tests with HLW AZ-101 feed

 31

with added sugar and a ruthenium spike [12]. Another sample from the test with the same
ruthenium spike but with the highest sugar concentration had a DF across the SBS of
only about 36; samples taken from tests with the same HLW AZ-101 feed spiked with
ruthenium but without sugar gave DF values of about 40 [7]; and samples taken from
tests with the same HLW AZ-101 feed spiked with ruthenium but with lower sugar
concentrations gave DF values of 25 and 58 [11]. The reason for the high measured DF
values for the unique combination of ruthenium and sugar is unclear. In keeping with
expectations that the SBS will remove coarser insoluble particles are the higher DF
values for silicon than sodium, as shown in Figure 5.2. Except for two early samples,
silicon DF values were typically one to two orders of magnitude higher than the total
solids DF, which was in turn typically the same or higher than the sodium DF.

 Modifications to the SBS did not result in discernable changes in the particle
removal efficiency, as shown in Figure 5.3. Configurations with most test data exhibit the
highest degree of variability in particle removal efficiency, suggesting that of the
configurations tested, the feed chemistry and melter conditions have a greater impact on
particle removal than any of the configuration changes. The measured removal efficiency
of the final SBS configuration is within the range of the values obtained for the other
configurations; however, this observation is only supported by data from two samples.

 Compositions representative of SBS emissions (excluding oxygen, carbon, nitrate,
and nitrite) for exhaust generated while processing HLW and LAW simulants are
displayed in Figures 5.4 and 5.5, respectively. Notice the high relative percentages of
volatiles, such as halides, selenium, and alkali metals as a result of the SBS removing the
vast majority of the insoluble components such as silicon, aluminum, and iron. Iodine,
when present in the feed, constitutes a large fraction of the SBS emissions as the result of
very low retention in the glass and poor iodine removal in the SBS. In tests processing
feeds without the iodine spike, the relative percentages of other halogens, alkali metals,
selenium, sulfur, and boron increase. The amount of halides in the SBS exhaust is under
represented due to the lack of sufficient sampled particulate to analyze for both metals
and particulate halides. Alkali metals constitute a larger proportion of the SBS emissions
in tests with LAW simulants due to their higher proportions in the melter feed. The HLW
AZ-101 simulant has a higher concentration of sulfur than the HLW C-106/AY-102
simulant (Figure 5.4) and therefore higher proportions of sulfur are observed in the SBS
while processing HLW AZ-101 simulants. Cesium was present in most SBS emission
samples in tests in which it was included in the feed; the measured DF for cesium was
between 1 and 17. Impinger solutions from off-gas sampling were analyzed for all of the
elements in the feed but only halides, boron, selenium, and sulfur were detected. Much of
the boron and selenium measured in the impinger solutions was probably not in the
gaseous form but was likely present as very fine particulate that passed through the 0.3-
µm heated filter. These results are consistent with expectations that melter emissions are
the result of both volatile constituents and feed entrainment; the SBS removes all but
some of the more volatile feed constituents, which form the majority of the finer
particulate matter and gaseous compounds.

 32

 Samples from select tests were taken using a University of Washington cascade
impactor, which separates particles into particle size ranges enabling the determination of
particle size distributions. The melter exhaust stream was sampled in several tests with
HLW and LAW simulants [5-12, 15-17], whereas the SBS exhaust was sampled only in
two early testing sequences while HLW AZ-101 simulants were processed [5, 6]. About
two-thirds of the total particulate mass in melter emissions from tests with HLW
simulants was of a size greater than about 10 µm, whereas 55 to 100 percent of total
particulate mass in SBS emissions from tests with HLW simulants was less than about
1 µm. These data are consistent with the design intent of removal of coarse material by
the SBS. Insufficient material was collected on each of the eight filter stages for chemical
analysis.

 33

 SECTION 6.0
 COMPARISON OF DM1200 SBS TO WTP DESIGN

 The design of the WTP HLW and LAW SBS is very similar to that of the
DM1200 SBS [23-28]. Specifically, the off-gas enters the top of the SBS and passes
downwards through a downcomer, which is centered in the SBS, to the diffuser plate.
The off-gas flow turns upwards and passes through the diffuser plate and into the
submerged packed bed where the off-gas is cooled. The off-gas continues up through the
packing into the plenum space where it then exits the SBS through a port on the top of the
SBS (see Figures 6.1 and 6.2). The submerged beds in the DM1200 and WTP LAW and
HLW SBSs are almost identical to one another except that the plenum heights (height
above the nominal liquid level) are slightly different. Figures 6.3 and 6.4 provide section
views of the bed assembly in the WTP HLW and LAW SBSs. It is not a coincidence that
the internals of the DM1200 SBS are nearly identical to the WTP HLW SBS. The last
modification made to the DM1200 SBS was to ensure that it was as prototypic to the
HLW SBS internals as possible. The only difference in the internal design between the
DM1200 and HLW SBSs is that the DM1200 SBS has only two sets of cooling coils
(similar to the WTP LAW SBS) as compared to the three sets in the WTP HLW SBS.
This slight deviation from the WTP HLW SBS design does not directly impact the
operation (i.e., differential pressure and gas cooling ability) of the SBS.

 Table 6.1 provides a comparison of the design parameters of the DM1200 and the
WTP SBSs. The DM1200 SBS utilizes the same packing material and maintains the same
liquid level above the packing as the WTP SBSs. Based on the exit flow values, the bed
superficial velocity for the WTP HLW SBS is about 35% larger than that of the DM1200
SBS while that for the WTP LAW SBS is larger still. The residence times in the packed
bed follow the reverse order. The diffuser plate on the DM1200 SBS is nearly identical to
the WTP SBSs (see Figures 6.5 – 6.7) except that it contains fewer liquid weirs and gas
slots. The gas flow per slot for the DM1200 SBS is lower than the corresponding values
for the WTP HLW and LAW SBSs.

 The inlet off-gas velocity in the DM1200 is somewhat higher (by about 22% and
5% for HLW and LAW, respectively) than those of the WTP SBSs. The impact of a
lower velocity on the operation of the WTP SBSs is not clear, especially on the potential
buildup of particulate matter in the downcomer section of the SBS.

 34

 SECTION 7.0
 SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS

 The SBS installed in the DM1200 off-gas system has been used in all DM1200
tests over the five-year testing period since the commissioning of the melter system. The
SBS has treated exhaust from the vitrification of over a million and half pounds of feed
producing over half a million pounds of glass. A wide range of simulated HLW and
LAW streams were treated under a variety of melter and off-gas system operating
conditions. During this period, a number of physical modifications to the SBS were made
and evaluated. From these tests, a large body of data has been collected on the
effectiveness of the SBS and on required maintenance activities and failure modes.

 A series of physical modifications to the SBS were made during the five year
testing program to preserve the prototypical relationship with the WTP SBS as design
changes were made in response to test results. One recurring issue was the formation of
solid deposits near the base of the downcomer at the bottom of the SBS packed column.
While these deposits never fully impeded gas flow through the SBS, over the course of
five- to nine-day tests they occluded as much as 80% of the pipe area and caused
increases in the pressure differential across the SBS. None of the modifications prior to
the final configuration, including various downcomer extensions, plugging weir tubes,
and insulating the downcomer above the water level, had any effect on the formation of
downcomer solids. The final SBS modification, which opened the annulus and shortened
the submerged portion of the downcomer, eliminated the accumulation of downcomer
deposits over the last 51 days of DM1200 testing. Further tests would be useful to verify
this observation using feeds of various compositions over a wide range of melter
operating conditions. Also, only two samples of SBS exhaust were taken after the final
modification of the SBS was completed; there are therefore only limited data on the
performance of the final configuration of the SBS unit.

 Throughout DM1200 testing, the SBS was periodically drained and inspected for
deposits and unusual wear. The most significant findings were accumulations of deposits
in the bottom of the SBS. There is no evidence that the rate of solids accumulation
declines as the test duration increases, as would be required in order to achieve a steady
state condition in which the rate of accumulation of solids is equal to the rate of removal.
Instead, the rate of solids removal showed an increasing trend with test duration. In
aggregate, therefore, the DM1200 test results indicate a build up of solids in the bottom
of the SBS over time. Accumulations of solids in the WTP SBS units have the potential
to interrupt operations. Further testing is needed to quantify the rate of solids
accumulations and to develop and demonstrate mitigation methods.

 Solutions are removed from the SBS throughout each test by suction through a
wand that extends to the bottom of the SBS bowl. Over the five-year period of testing,
solutions with total solids content as high as 10,000 mg/l were processed with only one

 35

clogging event. In solutions generated from tests processing HLW simulants, the amounts
of suspended and dissolved solids are about equal, whereas solutions generated while
processing LAW simulants have ten times higher concentrations of dissolved than
suspended solids. The composition of suspended solids is similar to that of the feed
composition whereas the dissolved solids consist primarily of the more volatile species.

 The effectiveness of the SBS in removing particulate ranged from DF values of 2
up to 122. Several factors have the potential to affect the efficiency of the SBS, including
the feed composition, melter operating conditions, and the rate of particle emissions from
the melter. Analysis of emissions data from tests with HLW simulants suggests that as
the flux of solids from the melter increases, so too does the SBS DF. This is not observed
in tests with LAW simulants due to the generation of higher concentrations of finer and
more soluble particles which are not as effectively removed by the SBS. In keeping with
the design of the SBS, the removal efficiencies for insoluble elements such as silicon and
iron are one or two orders of magnitude higher than those for soluble elements, such as
sodium, that form fine particles. The changes in SBS configuration that were made had
no discernable effect on particulate removal efficiency.

 A radar level gauge (Rosemont Apex Model BC1A1SC4NO4A1OC1N1E51) was
installed on the final SBS configuration. The limited testing that was performed indicated
relatively poor performance in this particular application. Many of the performance
problems that were encountered may be due to poor reflection of the radar beam from the
turbulent surface resulting in overall decrease in measurement sensitivity, perhaps
compounded by the small size and tight geometry of the DM1200 SBS. It is therefore
recommended that application of this technology to SBS level monitoring should be
