further evaluated prior to installation at the WTP.

 The designs of the WTP HLW and LAW SBSs are very similar to that of the final
configuration of the DM1200 SBS and, therefore, operational information obtained from
the DM1200 SBS during testing should be directly applicable to the WTP units.

 (1/7/02-1/15/02) After the test, blew down SBS and overflow tank. Total of 280 gallons of fluid were removed.
 Removed the SBS bowl. Inspection of SBS bowl showed very little residual solids and a small amount

 8/13/03)

 quadrant of the SBS bowl, the apparent dead spot in SBS bowl circulation. The bowl was cleaned of debris prior to the

 After Test #4, dry solids removed from the SBS overflow tank totaled ~25.4 kg.
 An IR video inspection was made of the SBS downcomer. There were fairly smooth, thin deposits at the top of the
 SBS downcomer at 30" down from the top of the viewport (at the bottom of the Y-inlet from the transition line). At
 about 66" down from the top viewport, the deposits were thinner than at the top of the downcomer, apparently
 becoming large brittle flakes, which ended at this depth. There was a ring of deposits, which blocked approximately
 7% of the flow area near the base of the downcomer. The calculated average height of this ridge was 1/8 ". The blow-
 down wand was approximately half-buried in solids in the SBS bowl. This is the likely cause, at least in part, for the
 discharge pressure gauge readings of the SBS recirculation pump, which indicated decreasing pressure. In response to
 None 8/29/2003 this, the operators partially closed the flow valve, which manually controls the recirculation rate in order to maintain a
 constant flow of around 20 gpm. However, the consequence of the reduced circulation was probably lower turbulence
 and more settling of solids in the SBS bowl. The pressure gauge was recalibrated at the conclusion of this test, and
 presumably the turbulence was restored within the SBS bowl during the following test (Test 10). Since a large volume
 of solids was recovered at the conclusion of that test, apparently, once solids settle in the SBS bowl, they cannot be
 easily re-suspended.
 Recalibrated the SBS recirculation pump pressure gauge. Inspection of suction pipe opening for SBS pump, SBS-P-
 501, showed solids build-up all the way to its opening. Solids were removed from the tank, and the opening was no
 longer blocked. Replenished SBS and SBS overflow tank.

 [11] sulfate A problem had been noted with the SBS recirculation pump during the previous test (Test 4) in that the SBS
 (9/3/03- internal circulation had been greatly reduced. It would be expected that reduction of circulation would cause an

 of solids had already started during Test 3 in mid-July, prior to Test 4 when the pump problems were first
 noticed. It is, however, possible that the recirculation pump problem started during Test 3, but was only noticed
 later during Test 4. The recirculation pump was fully functional for the last of these tests.
 The above observations suggest that once particulates settle in the SBS bowl and stay there for a significant
 time, they will likely not be re-suspended by the pump. Operators removing these SBS bowl solids after Test
 10 noted that the deposits were very hard to remove.

 line had a thin (~1/16" thick) uniform layer of deposits. Typically, this inlet has quite a noticeable build-up of
 deposits along the bottom of the transition line. As is typical of all SBS inspections, on the downcomer
 immediately below the Y-inlet there were deposits/debris that had apparently rolled along the bottom of the
 transition-line. This debris covered perhaps one fourth of the inside perimeter of the upper half of the
 downcomer. After most runs, we have noticed smooth, thin deposits (or thin, smooth flakes) in the upper half
 LAW of the downcomer, with an additional buildup immediately below the Y-inlet. In this run, other than the debris
 Envelopes A track, the top half of the downcomer was clean. As was typical of SBS inspections, there was a double ring of
    VSL-04R4851-1
 and C 10/24/2003 solids, perhaps 1/8" high and separated by 3" to 4", about 5'6" down. The bottom half of the downcomer was

 VSL-
 4/02/04) Added ~56 gallons of city water to the SBS overflow tank. No leaks were detected. Also, successfully tested the low level
 04R4800-4
 HLW 3/11/2004 interlock switch with SBS recirculation pump, SBS-PW-P-501. This pump would not run until the low level switch probe
 [11]
 AZ-101 is covered with water.
 (3/23/04-
 SBS piping modifications were completed. About 30 gallons of ethylene glycol was added to the SBS cooling system to
 4/02/04)
 raise the concentration of ethylene glycol in the system. Prior to these tests, a modified SBS was placed in service. Details
 of the SBS modifications are given in a previous report [11]. A summary of the significant modifications to the SBS are as
 follows:
 • The diameter of the bed was increased by 40% (outer vessel inner diameter of 47½”) to make the superficial flow
 velocity closer to its prototypical value.
 • The water level above the packing was lowered by several inches such that the nominal level is now equal to the
 prototypical level of 3 inches above the top of the packing. This change reduces the pressure drop across the SBS. It
 3/16/2004 also results in a prototypical SBS plenum height (21 inches) from the top of the liquid to the off-gas entry and exit
 points. The DM1200 SBS plenum volume is, however, smaller than prototypical due to the difference in SBS
 diameters.
 • The overflow nozzle design was changed to the prototypical funnel-like shape.
 • The down comer annular pipe was changed to the prototypical open-ended type.
 • An additional ring of the inner cooling coil was added, making a total of two coils in the DM1200 system as compared
 to three in the WTP SBS. This change improves the heat transfer efficiency enabling the support of higher (water)
 feed rates to the melter.
 SBS piping modifications for the cooling water system were pressure tested, and found to have no leaks.
